the worldline, i.e. the (phase space) trajectory of a point particle in
{\cal M}
.
Under the transformation generated by the gauge parameter
\lambda\in\Omega^{0}(I,\mathfrak{g})
,

\delta_{\lambda}y^{\mu}=\{\chi^{*}(\lambda),y^{\mu}\}\,,\qquad\delta_{\lambda}A=\mathrm{d}\lambda+[A,\lambda]\,,

(3.21)

the action transforms as

\delta_{\lambda}S[y,A]=\int_{I}\mathrm{d}\,\big(i_{\lambda}\,\vartheta(y)-\langle\mu(y),\lambda\rangle\big)+\langle\phi,\mathrm{d}\lambda+[A,\lambda]\rangle\,.

(3.22)

Up to a total derivative,
the above reduces to the integral of
\langle\phi,[A,\lambda]\rangle

and it vanishes only when
\lambda

takes value in the isotropy subalgebra
\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}
.
This shows that only
the first class constraints associated with
\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}

lead to gauge symmetries.
Under a finite gauge transformation
h\in G_{\phi}
,

y\ \to\ y^{h}=h^{-1}\triangleright y\,,\qquad A\ \to\ A^{h}=h^{-1}(A+\mathrm{d})h\,,

(3.23)

the action changes as

\displaystyle S[y^{h},A^{h}]-S[y,A]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{I}\vartheta(y^{h})-\vartheta(y)-\langle\chi,\mathrm{d}h\,h^{-1}\rangle

(3.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{I}\vartheta(y^{h})-\vartheta(y)-\langle\mu(y),\mathrm{d}h\,h^{-1}\rangle+\langle\phi,h^{-1}\mathrm{d}h\rangle\,,

where the first three terms are the finite counterpart of the total derivative

\mathrm{d}(i_{\lambda}\vartheta-\langle\mu(y),\lambda\rangle)
appearing for
infinitesimal gauge transformations.
The invariance of
\exp(\frac{i}{\hbar}\,S)
under the above transformation
requires that the last term be proportional to
2\pi\,\hbar
times an integer.
This leads precisely to the same quantisation condition on
\phi
as in (
2.37
). One can also convert all the second class constraints into first class ones
by introducing additional variables: see Appendix
B
.

3.3
Example: Cotangent bundle of a Lie group

An important class of examples of the above discussion
is the cotangent bundle
{\cal M}=T^{*}G
of a Lie group
G
,
which is a symplectic manifold as any cotangent bundle:
Locally, the symplectic form reads

\Omega_{T^{*}G}=\mathrm{d}\vartheta\,,\qquad\vartheta=p_{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\,,

(3.25)

where
\{x^{\mu}\}
are coordinates on
G
and

\{p_{\mu}\}
are coordinates in the fiber
directions of
T^{*}G
. The Lie group
G
acts
on its algebra of functions via left- or right-
invariant vector fields, which will be denoted by

\rho_{a}=\rho_{a}{}^{\mu}(x)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\in\Gamma(TG)\,,

(3.26)

and where the Latin index
a
refers to
a basis
\{J_{a}\}
of the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}
. These vector fields can be
lifted to functions on
T^{*}G
via

\mu_{a}(x,p):=\rho_{a}{}^{\mu}(x)\,p_{\mu}\,,

(3.27)

which verifies

\{\mu_{a},\mu_{b}\}=f_{ab}{}^{c}\,\mu_{c}\,,

(3.28)

where
\{-,-\}
denotes the Poisson bracket
associated with the symplectic two-form
(
3.25
). With this data,
we can consider a constrained Hamiltonian
system of the type described previously,
whose corresponding worldline is given by

S[x,p,A]=\int p_{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}-A^{a}\left(\rho_{a}{}^{\mu}(x)\,p_{\mu}-\phi_{a}\right)\,.

(3.29)

The constraints

\chi_{a}(x,p)=\rho_{a}{}^{\mu}(x)\,p_{\mu}-\phi_{a}\approx 0\,,

(3.30)

can be solved simply by

p_{\mu}=e_{\mu}^{a}(x)\,\phi_{a}\,,

(3.31)

where
e_{\mu}^{a}(x)
are the components of
the left-invariant Maurer–Cartan form of
G
,

g(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}g(x)=e^{a}_{\mu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\,J_{a}\in\Omega^{1}(G,\mathfrak{g})\,,

(3.32)

which are the inverse of the components
of the left-invariant vector fields.
Inserting the solution of the constraints
in the action, we recover the expression,

S[x]=\int\phi_{a}\,e^{a}_{\mu}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}=\int\langle\phi,g(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}g(x)\rangle\,.

(3.33)

Remark that the constraint surface is

\mu^{-1}(\phi)=\{(g,{\rm Ad}_{g}^{*}\phi)\in T^{*}G\,|\,g\in G\}\cong G\,,

(3.34)

where we used the fact that the cotangent bundle
of a Lie group is trivial,

T^{*}G\cong G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.
Further quotienting by the gauge symmetry generated by
the first class constraints, which is given by
the action of the isotropy group
G_{\phi}
, leads to

{\cal N}_{\phi}=\mu^{-1}(\phi)/G_{\phi}\cong{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}\,,

(3.35)

i.e. the reduced phase space is nothing but
the coadjoint orbit of
\phi
.
15
15

15

In this simple case,
we can also verify the Marsden–Meyers–Weinstein
theorem explicitly. To do so, let us note that,
under the trivialization provided by the Maurer–Cartan
form, the tautological form on the cotangent bundle

T^{*}G\cong G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
reads

\vartheta\rvert_{(g,\varphi)}=\langle\varphi,\Theta_{g}\rangle

implying
\Omega_{T^{*}G}\rvert_{(g,\varphi)}=\mathrm{d}\langle\varphi,\Theta_{g}\rangle

at any point
(g,\varphi)\in G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
,
and where
\Theta\in\Omega^{1}(G,\mathfrak{g})

is the left-invariant Maurer–Cartan form of
G
.
We can now compare the pullback of the symplectic form
on the reduced phase space, which is the coadjoint orbit

{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}
, by the projection

\pi_{\phi}:G\twoheadrightarrow{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}:g\mapsto{\rm Ad}_{g}^{*}\phi
,
with the pullback of the symplectic form on the phase space

T^{*}G\cong G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
by the inclusion

\iota_{\phi}:G\hookrightarrow G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}:g\mapsto(g,\phi)
. We already computed the first
pullback in Section
2.2
, while the second one
simply amounts to the evaluation of
\Omega_{T^{*}G}

at
\phi\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, so that we find

\pi_{\phi}^{*}\Omega_{{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}}=\mathrm{d}_{G}\langle\phi,\Theta\rangle=\iota_{\phi}^{*}\Omega_{T^{*}G}
as expected.

Applying the general story (
3.15
) to this case,
we find

T^{*}G\cong\bigcup_{\varphi\in\Psi}G_{\varphi}\times\bigcup_{\phi\in\Phi_{\varphi}}{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}\times{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}\,.

(3.36)

Note that the quantisation of the above,
in the case when the Lie group
G
is compact,
leads to the Peter–Weyl theorem,

L^{2}(G)=\bigoplus_{\lambda}\pi^{G}_{\lambda}\otimes(\pi^{G}_{\lambda})^{*}\,,

(3.37)

where
\pi_{\lambda}
is the unitary irreducible
representations of
G
labelled by
\lambda

(its highest weight, since
G
is assumed compact
here). The above decomposition of
T^{*}G

can be recovered as its orbit space under the action
of
G\times G
where the first factor acts
from the left and the second from the right
(see e.g.
[
134
]
for a recent discussion
in that direction).
In the next section, we shall see a similar pattern
of decompositions, but involving coadjoint orbits
of two different Lie groups.

4
Manifestly covariant formulation of coadjoint orbit action

In this section, we will explain how a mechanical system
given by a coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}
of a Lie group
G

can be realised as a constrained Hamiltonian system,
where the constraints are associated with a different
coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}

of a different Lie group
\tilde{G}
. Remark that
the analysis of the previous section applies to
\tilde{G}
,
associated with the
gauge
symmetry of the system,
while
G
is the
global
symmetry.
We will show that the Lie groups
G
and
\tilde{G}

are dual in the sense of
symplectic dual pairs

à la Weinstein
[
3
]

(see also
[
135
, Chap. 4]

for a textbook account). The quantised picture
corresponds to Howe duality
[
4
,
5
]
,
also known as dual pair correspondence
(see also e.g.
[
136
,
137
,
138
,
91
,
92
]
).

The construction below can be understood
as a method of obtaining a good coordinate system
for the coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}
,
along the lines of the discussion
in
2.5
. More precisely,
we want to reformulate the system in such a way
that its global symmetries are manifest. In other words,
we want the global symmetry to be realised linearly,
as opposed to a nonlinear realisation,
so that all the phase space variables
carry faithful representations of the global symmetry.
This can be achieved by using the definition
of various matrix groups as Hamiltonian constraints.
In this set up, the phase space variables carry
the defining representations of the global symmetry,
as well as a representation of a gauge symmetry group.
We will find an exquisite relation between
the global and gauge symmetries of the system.

In the first two subsections
4.1

and
4.2
we present the construction
of worldline action for all classical Lie groups
and their semi-direct product with Abelian ideals.
The treatment here will be rather brief
as we consider the case of indefinite orthogonal group
and inhomogeneous orthogonal group in detail
in the subsections
4.3

and
4.4
.
The other cases will be detailed
in the sequel paper
[
93
]
,
along with twistor descriptions.
The readers who wish to focus on the case
of (inhomogeneous) orthogonal groups may skip
the first two subsections.

4.1
Classical Lie groups

Let us consider the classical Lie groups

GL(N,\mathbb{F})
,
U(p,N-p)
,
O(p,N-p)
,

O^{*}(2N)
,
O(N,\mathbb{C})
,
Sp(2N,\mathbb{R})
,

Sp(p,N-p)
and
Sp(2N,\mathbb{C})
,
where,
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
,
or the quaternion
\mathbb{H}
. They are all reductive,
and include many physically relevant cases,
such as Lorentz groups and (A)dS isometry groups:
the family
O(p,N-p)
contains the AdS
d
symmetry

O(2,d-1)
and the dS
d
symmetry
O(1,d)
.
The double covers of the orthochronous Lorentz groups

SO^{+}(2,1)
,
SO^{+}(3,1)
and
SO^{+}(5,1)

are isomorphic to
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
,
SL(2,\mathbb{C})

and
SL(2,\mathbb{H})
, the simple parts of
GL(2,\mathbb{F})
.
The double covers of the conformal groups
SO^{+}(2,3)
,

SO^{+}(2,4)
and
SO^{+}(2,6)
are isomorphic to
Sp(4,\mathbb{R})
,

SU(2,2)
and
O^{*}(8)
.

All classical Lie groups are subgroups of a general linear
group
GL(N,\mathbb{F})
with
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
,
defined by a quadratic equation — expressing the fact
that they preserve a certain bilinear form. We can introduce
most of them in a unified fashion as in
[
35
]
:
let us define

B(b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)},\mathbb{F})=\{\,A\in GL(N,\mathbb{F})\mid A^{\dagger}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,A=b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,\}\,,

(4.1)

where
b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}
is an element of
GL(N,\mathbb{F})
,
and
A^{\dagger}=(A^{t})^{*}
with
t
the matrix transpose
and
*
the conjugation of
\mathbb{F}
, which is the identity map
for
\mathbb{R}
, the complex conjugation for
\mathbb{C}
,
and the quaternionic conjugation for
\mathbb{H}
.
Up to a
GL(N,\mathbb{F})
transformation

b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\to T^{\dagger}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,T
,
we have essentially two possibilities:
either it is Hermitian or anti-Hermitian.
The Hermitian ones are equivalent
to
b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}=\eta^{\scriptscriptstyle(p,N-p)}
,
the standard flat metric of
(p,N-p)
signature.
The anti-Hermitian ones are equivalent
to
b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}=\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}
,
the canonical symplectic matrix.
See e.g.
[
105
, Prop. 9.3.2.]

for relevant discussions. The group
B(b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)},\mathbb{F})

either simply coincides with one of classical Lie groups
or is isomorphic to it:

\displaystyle B(\eta^{\scriptscriptstyle(p,N-p)},\mathbb{R})=O(p,N-p)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle B(\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)},\mathbb{R})=Sp(N,\mathbb{R})\,,

(4.2)

\displaystyle B(\eta^{\scriptscriptstyle(p,N-p)},\mathbb{C})=U(p,N-p)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle B(\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)},\mathbb{C})\simeq U(\tfrac{N}{2},\tfrac{N}{2})\,,

(4.3)

\displaystyle B(\eta^{\scriptscriptstyle(p,N-p)},\mathbb{H})=Sp(p,N-p)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle B(\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)},\mathbb{H})\simeq O^{*}(2N)\,.

(4.4)

In the right column, the cases with
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}

and
\mathbb{C}
are defined only for even
N
.
For the case
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}
,
N
can be both even
and odd because the second element of
\mathbb{H}

can be seen as a two-dimensional symplectic matrix
so we can take
\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}=\mathbf{j}\,I_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}
.
Note that
B(\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)},\mathbb{C})
are isomorphic
to unitary groups because we can diagonalize
\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}

as
i\,\eta^{\scriptscriptstyle(N/2,N/2)}
. On the contrary,

B(\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)},\mathbb{H})
is not isomorphic to
Sp(N/2,N/2)

but
O^{*}(2N)
, even though
\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}
can still be diagonalised
as
\mathbf{i}\,\eta^{\scriptscriptstyle(N/2,N/2)}
. It is because
\mathbf{i}
,
the first element of the basis of
\mathbb{H}
,
does not commute with a quaternionic matrix.

We can also define another class of classical Lie groups
by using the transpose
t
at the place of the Hermitian conjugate
in the definition (
4.1
). Then, for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
,
this trivially coincides with
B(b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)},\mathbb{R})
.
For
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}
, this fails to form a group.
Only
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
, it defines a new classical Lie group,

C(b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)})=\{A\in GL(N,\mathbb{C})\,|\,A^{t}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,A=b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\}\,.

(4.5)

Again, up to a
GL(N,\mathbb{C})
transformation,
we have two possibilities,
b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}=I_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}

and
b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}=\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}
, corresponding to

C(I_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)})=O(N,\mathbb{C})\,,\qquad C(\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)})=Sp(N,\mathbb{C})\,.

(4.6)

The latter case is defined only for even
N
.
Note that these Lie groups are not simple
as a real Lie group, but semisimple.

On the manifold
GL(N,\mathbb{F})
, the components
of
X^{a}{}_{b}\in\mathbb{F}
of an element
X\in GL(N,\mathbb{F})

serve a natural and global coordinate system,
and a left-invariant vector field is given by

V=V^{a}{}_{b}\,X^{c}{}_{a}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial X^{c}{}_{b}}
.
The action takes the simple form,

S[X,P,A]=\frac{1}{2}\int{\rm Tr}_{N\times N}\left[P\,\mathrm{d}X+A\,(P\,X-\phi)+(\rm conj)\right],

(4.7)

where all three fields
X
,
P
and
A
as well as
\phi

take value in
Mat_{N\times N}(\mathbb{F})
,
and the symbol (conj) stands for the conjugate in
\mathbb{F}
.
Remark that adding the conjugate is trivial for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}

as it duplicates the Lagrangian and only replaces
the factor
\tfrac{1}{2}
by
1
in the end.
For
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}

complementing the Lagrangian with the conjugate
is necessary to recover the
2(2N)^{2}
and
2(4N)^{2}
dimensional
symplectic potentials. Note that we can solve the constraint
algebraically to get

S[X]=\frac{1}{2}\int{\rm Tr}_{N\times N}\left[\phi\,X^{-1}\mathrm{d}X+({\rm conj})\right],

(4.8)

which is nothing but a matrix form of (
3.33
).

If the coadjoint element
\phi
is a rank
M
matrix with
M\leq N
,
the above action can be reduced to
16
16

16

To be more
precise, this reduction requires integrating out
non-dynamical variables corresponding
to the components of
X
in the subspace

Mat_{N\times(N-M)}(\mathbb{F})
.

S[X,P,A]=\frac{1}{2}\int{\rm Tr}_{M\times M}\left[P\,\mathrm{d}X+A\,(P\,X-\tilde{\phi})+{(\rm conj)}\right],

(4.9)

where the fields
X
,
P
and
A
takes value
in
Mat_{N\times M}(\mathbb{F})
,

Mat_{M\times N}(\mathbb{F})

and
Mat_{M\times M}(\mathbb{F})
, respectively.
Here
\tilde{\phi}
also belongs to
Mat_{M\times M}(\mathbb{F})

and it is the
M\times M
submatrix of a triangulation of
\phi
.
The resulting action describes a
G=GL(N,\mathbb{F})

coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}
as a reduced phase space
inside
\mathbb{F}^{2MN}
where the constraints
are given by the moment maps

\tilde{\mu}(X,P)=P\,X\in Mat_{M\times M}(\mathbb{F})

generating
\tilde{G}=GL(M,\mathbb{F})
under Poisson bracket.
Note also that the moment map

\mu(X,P)=X\,P\in Mat_{N\times N}(\mathbb{F})

associated with the original
GL(N,\mathbb{F})
symmetry
commutes with
\tilde{\mu}
. The constraints

\tilde{\chi}(X,P)=\tilde{\mu}(X,P)-\tilde{\phi}\approx 0

are associated with the
\tilde{G}=GL(M,\mathbb{F})

coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}
.

Now, let us move to the classical Lie groups

B(b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)},\mathbb{F})
. Adding the definition (
4.1
)
to the action (
4.8
) as a constraint,
we start with the action

\displaystyle S[X,A]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\frac{1}{2}{\rm Tr}_{N\times N}\left[\left(\phi\,X^{-1}\mathrm{d}X\right)+A\,(X^{\dagger}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,X-b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)})+({\rm conj})\right]

(4.10)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\int{\rm Tr}_{N\times N}\left[\left(\phi\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}^{\ \ -1}\,X^{\dagger}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,\mathrm{d}X\right)+A\,(X^{\dagger}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,X-b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)})+({\rm conj})\right]\,,

where
\cong
means the equivalence up to a redefinition of
A
.
For any
\phi
, there exists a
T\in Mat_{N\times M}(\mathbb{F})

and
\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}
with
M\leq N
such that

\phi=T\,\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}\,T^{\dagger}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,.

(4.11)

Here,
\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}=\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}

for
b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}=\eta^{\scriptscriptstyle(p,N-p)}
,
and
\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}=\eta^{\scriptscriptstyle(q,M-q)}

for
b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}=\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}
.
With a suitable redefinition of
X
and
A
in terms of
T
,
we can express the action as

S[X,A]=\int\frac{1}{2}{\rm Tr}_{M\times M}\left[\left(\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}\,X^{\dagger}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,\mathrm{d}X\right)+A\left(X^{\dagger}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,X-\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}^{\ \ -1}\,\tilde{\phi}\right)+{\rm(conj)}\right],

(4.12)

where
X
takes value in
Mat_{N\times M}(\mathbb{F})
and

\tilde{\phi}\in Mat_{M\times M}
is given by

\tilde{\phi}=\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}\,T^{\dagger}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,T\,.

(4.13)

Note that the moment maps
\tilde{\mu}(X)=X^{\dagger}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,X\in Mat_{M\times M}(\mathbb{F})

generates the dual symmetry

\tilde{G}=B(\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)},\mathbb{F})
whereas

\mu(X)=X\,\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}\,X^{\dagger}\in Mat_{N\times N}(\mathbb{F})

generates the original symmetry
G=B(b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)},\mathbb{F})
.

The classical Lie group
C(b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)})
can be treated
in a very similar manner. We find

S[X,A]=\int\frac{1}{2}\,{\rm Tr}_{M\times M}\left[\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}\,X^{t}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,\mathrm{d}X+A\left(X^{t}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,X-\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}^{\ \ -1}\,\tilde{\phi}\right)+({\rm conj})\right]\,,

(4.14)

where the dual coadjoint vector
\tilde{\phi}
is related to
the coadjoint vector
\phi
through

\phi=T\,\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}\,T^{t}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)},\qquad\tilde{\phi}=\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}\,T^{t}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,T\,,

(4.15)

with a
T\in Mat_{N\times M}(\mathbb{C})
.
Here,
\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}=\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}

for
b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}=I_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}
,
and
\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}=I_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}

for
b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}=\Omega_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}
.
The moment maps
\tilde{\mu}(X)=X^{t}\,b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)}\,X\in Mat_{M\times M}(\mathbb{C})

generate the dual symmetry
\tilde{G}=C(\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)})

whereas
\mu(X)=X\,\tilde{b}_{\scriptscriptstyle(M)}\,X^{t}\in Mat_{N\times N}(\mathbb{C})

generates the original symmetry
G=C(b_{\scriptscriptstyle(N)})
.

4.2
Semi-direct product group

The above construction can be extended to a class
of non-reductive Lie groups
G
, which are given by
semi-direct product
G=I\rtimes H
between a reductive group
H

treated above, and an Abelian ideal
I
carries a
H
-representation
\pi
.
Any element
g
of
G
can be denoted by
g=i\,h
with
h\in H

and
i\in I
. The semi-direct product rule can be deduced
from
h\,i=(\pi(h)\,i)\,h
. The adjoint and coadjoint actions
of an element
(a,h)\in G
read

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}_{(e^{a},h)}(\chi,\xi)=\big(\pi(h)(\chi+\pi(\xi)\,a),\,{\rm Ad}_{h}\xi\big)\,,

\displaystyle{\rm Ad}^{*}_{(e^{a},h)}(\phi_{I},\phi_{H})=\big(\pi^{*}(h)\phi_{I},\,\langle\pi^{*}(h)\phi_{I},\pi(-)\,a\rangle_{\mathfrak{i}}+{\rm Ad}^{*}_{h}\phi_{H}\big)\,,

(4.16)

where
\langle\phi_{I},\pi(-)\,a\rangle_{\mathfrak{i}}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}

is defined such that for any
\xi\in\mathfrak{h}
,

\big\langle\langle\phi_{I},\pi(-)\,a\rangle_{\mathfrak{i}},\xi\big\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}=\langle\phi_{I},\pi(\xi)\,a\rangle_{\mathfrak{i}}\,.

(4.17)

From the coadjoint action

{\rm Ad}^{*}_{(i,{\rm id})}(\phi_{I},\phi_{H})=(\phi_{I},\langle\phi_{I},\pi(-)\,a\rangle_{\mathfrak{i}}+\phi_{H})

and the property
\big\langle\langle\phi_{I},\pi(-)\,a\rangle_{\mathfrak{i}}+\phi_{H},\xi\big\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}=-\langle\pi^{*}(\xi)\,\phi_{I},a\rangle_{\mathfrak{i}}+\langle\phi_{H},\xi\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}
,
we can see that, if
\xi
does not belong to the little group algebra
\mathfrak{h}_{\phi_{I}}
of
\phi_{I}
,
we can set
\langle\phi_{H},\xi\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}
to zero with a suitable choice of
a
. This means that a coadjoint vector
(\phi_{I},\phi_{H})
can be
always rotated in a way that
\phi_{H}
belongs to

\mathfrak{h}_{\phi_{I}}^{*}
.
It is also known that a coadjoint orbit
of a semi-direct product group has the structure
of a fiber bundle with the ‘momentum orbit’,
i.e. the orbit of
H
on
\phi_{I}
, as the base manifold
and the direct product of the cotangent space
of the momentum orbit times the coadjoint orbit of
\phi_{H}

under the ‘little group’
H_{\phi_{I}}
as the fiber,
see e.g.
[
139
,
140
]

or
[
141
,
142
]
and references therein.

The Maurer–Cartan element reads

g^{-1}\,{\rm d}_{G}\,g=(e^{a}\,h)^{-1}\,{\rm d}_{G}\,(e^{a}\,h)=\pi(h^{-1})\,{\rm d}_{I}\,a+h^{-1}{\rm d}_{H}\,h\,,

(4.18)

and the coadjoint orbit action is

S[g]=\int\langle\phi,g^{-1}\,{\rm d}_{G}\,g\rangle_{\mathfrak{g}}=\int\left[\langle\phi_{I},\pi(h^{-1})\,{\rm d}_{I}\,a\rangle_{\mathfrak{i}}+\langle\phi_{H},h^{-1}{\rm d}_{H}\,h\rangle_{\mathfrak{h}}\right]\,,

(4.19)

where the coadjoint vector
\phi
is split
into
\phi=\phi_{H}+\phi_{I}
with
\phi_{H}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}

and
\phi_{I}\in\mathfrak{i}^{*}
.
We can treat the second part of the action
as in the subsection
4.1
.

4.3
Orthogonal groups

In this section, we reconsider the coadjoint orbit actions
of the indefinite orthogonal groups
O(p,N-p)
with more details.
From the definition, the action is given by

\displaystyle S[X,A]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int{\rm Tr}_{N\times N}\left[\phi\,X^{-1}\,{\rm d}X+A\,(X^{t}\,\eta\,X-\eta)\right]

(4.20)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\int{\rm Tr}_{N\times N}\left[\phi\,\eta^{-1}\,X^{t}\,\eta\,{\rm d}X+A\,(X^{t}\,\eta\,X-\eta)\right],

where
\eta
is the flat metric of
(p,N-p)
signature.
The matrix
\phi\,\eta^{-1}
is antisymmetric,
and suppose that its rank is
2M\leq N
.
Then we can always find a rectangular matrix

T^{a}{}_{\beta}\in Mat_{N\times M}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\phi^{ab}=T^{a}{}_{\alpha}\,T^{b}{}_{\beta}\,\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,,

(4.21)

where
\Omega^{\alpha\beta}
is the symplectic matrix of rank
2M\leq N

and
\phi^{ab}=(\phi\,\eta^{-1})^{ab}=\phi^{a}{}_{c}\,\eta^{cb}

Here, the indices
a,b=1,\ldots,N
while
\alpha,\beta=1,\ldots,2M
.
We can append to
T
a
N\times(N-2M)
matrix
R

so that they jointly form a matrix
(T\,R)\in GL(N,\mathbb{R})
.
Introducing the indices
\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta}=2M+1,\ldots,N
,
we can consider the redefinition,

A=(T\,R)\,A^{\prime}\,(T\,R)^{t}
or in components

A^{ab}=A^{\prime\alpha\beta}\,T^{a}{}_{\alpha}\,T^{b}{}_{\beta}+A^{\prime\bar{\alpha}\beta}\,R^{a}{}_{\bar{\alpha}}\,T^{b}{}_{\beta}+A^{\prime\alpha\bar{\beta}}\,T^{a}{}_{\alpha}\,R^{b}_{\bar{\beta}}+A^{\prime\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}}\,R^{a}{}_{\bar{\alpha}}\,R^{b}{}_{\bar{\beta}}\,,

(4.22)

then also

X^{\prime a}{}_{\alpha}=X^{a}{}_{b}\,T^{b}{}_{\alpha}\,,\qquad\bar{X}^{\prime a}{}_{\bar{\alpha}}=X^{a}{}_{b}\,R^{b}{}_{\bar{\alpha}}\,.

(4.23)

By removing the prime from the variables,
the action can be written as

\displaystyle S[X,A]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,X^{a}{}_{\beta}\,{\rm d}X_{a\alpha}+A^{\alpha\beta}\left(X_{c\beta}\,X^{c}{}_{\alpha}-\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha\beta}\right)

(4.24)

\displaystyle\quad+\,2\,A^{\alpha\bar{\beta}}\left(\bar{X}_{c\bar{\beta}}\,X^{c}{}_{\alpha}-\varphi_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}\right)+A^{\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}}\left(\bar{X}_{c\bar{\beta}}\,\bar{X}^{c}{}_{\bar{\alpha}}-\varphi_{\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}}\right),

where the Latin indices are lowered by
\eta_{ab}

and
\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\varphi_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}
,
and
\varphi_{\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}}
are given by

\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha\beta}=T^{a}{}_{\alpha}\,T_{a\beta}\,,\quad\varphi_{\alpha\bar{\beta}}=T^{a}{}_{\alpha}\,R_{a\bar{\beta}}\,,\quad\varphi_{\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}}=R^{a}{}_{\bar{\alpha}}\,R_{a\bar{\beta}}\,.

(4.25)

The constraints given by
A^{\alpha\bar{\beta}}
and
A^{\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}}

can be algebraically solved for the variables
\bar{X}^{a}{}_{\bar{\alpha}}
,
and this results in a constant factor. Discarding this factor,
the final form of the action is simply

S[X,A]=\int\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,X^{a}{}_{\beta}\,{\rm d}X_{a\alpha}+A^{\alpha\beta}\left(X_{c\beta}\,X^{c}{}_{\alpha}-\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha\beta}\right)\,.

(4.26)

The constraints are given by
the momentum maps
\tilde{\mu}_{\beta\alpha}=X_{c\beta}\,X^{c}{}_{\alpha}

closed under the Poisson bracket as

\{\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha\beta},\tilde{\mu}_{\gamma\delta}\}=\Omega_{\beta\gamma}\,\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha\delta}+\Omega_{\alpha\gamma}\,\tilde{\mu}_{\beta\delta}+\Omega_{\alpha\delta}\,\tilde{\mu}_{\beta\gamma}+\Omega_{\beta\delta}\,\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha\gamma}\,,

(4.27)

and hence defines the dual Lie algebra

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sp}(2M,\mathbb{R})
.
Since the constraints
\tilde{\chi}_{\alpha\beta}=\tilde{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}-\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha\beta}\approx 0

are given with a constant shift

\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha\beta}
,
they are a mixture of the first
and the second class constraints.
According to the general results
presented in Section
3.2
,
the first class constraints
are the linear combinations

\tilde{\chi}^{*}(\xi)=\xi^{\alpha\beta}\,\tilde{\chi}_{\alpha\beta}

satisfying

\{\tilde{\chi}^{*}(\xi),\tilde{\chi}_{\gamma\delta}\}\,\approx\,2\,\xi^{\alpha\beta}(\Omega_{\beta\gamma}\,\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha\delta}+\Omega_{\beta\delta}\,\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha\gamma})=0\qquad\forall\gamma,\,\delta\,.

(4.28)

This forms
a subalgebra

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}\subset\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sp}(2M,\mathbb{R})
,
whose structure
is determined by
\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha\beta}

hence by
\phi^{ab}
.
The matrix

\tilde{\phi}_{\alpha\beta}=\Omega_{\alpha\gamma}\,\tilde{\phi}^{\gamma}{}_{\beta}

then
\tilde{\phi}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta}

corresponds to the coadjoint vector of
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}=\mathfrak{sp}(2M,\mathbb{R})^{*}
.
The remaining constraints
are the second class ones
corresponding to
the dual coadjoint orbit

{\cal O}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\tilde{G}/\tilde{G}_{\tilde{\phi}}
.

This construction clearly exhibits
the intimate relation between

\phi^{a}{}_{b}
and
\tilde{\phi}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta}
:
they are both given by
two different contractions of
T^{a}{}_{\beta}
:

\phi^{a}{}_{b}=T^{a}{}_{\alpha}\,\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,T^{c}{}_{\beta}\,\eta_{cb}\,,\qquad\tilde{\phi}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta}=\Omega^{\alpha\gamma}\,T^{a}{}_{\gamma}\,\eta_{ab}\,T^{b}{}_{\beta}\,.

(4.29)

Here, it is worth emphasising that the components
\tilde{\phi}^{\alpha}{}_{\beta}

are determined by
\phi^{a}{}_{b}
,
up to a
Sp(2M,\mathbb{R})
transformation.
The choice of a coadjoint element
\phi^{a}{}_{b}
itself is also fixed up to a
O(p,N-p)
transformation.
In other words, the choice of the matrix
T^{a}{}_{\beta}
is determined up to a
O(p,N-p)\times Sp(2M,\mathbb{R})
transformation.
In matrix form,
the relations (
4.29
) read

\phi=T\,\Omega\,T^{t}\,\eta,\qquad\tilde{\phi}=\Omega\,T^{t}\,\eta\,T\,,

(4.30)

and they satisfy the same invariant equations,

{\rm Tr}(\phi^{n})=I_{n}={\rm Tr}(\tilde{\phi}^{n})\,,

(4.31)

for any natural number
n
.
Allowing
\phi
and
\tilde{\phi}
to vary,
the above are polynomial functions in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}

and
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
respectively,
which commute (with respect to the Poisson bracket)
with any other functions, i.e. they are Casimir functions
of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}

respectively. The previous identity therefore
tells us that evaluating these Casimir functions
on two dual coadjoint orbits of
G
and
\tilde{G}

yields the same result. This is another ‘classical’
counterpart of a feature present in the dual pair correspondence:
the values of the Casimir operators of two dual groups,
on a pair of representations which are dual to one another,
are related
[
143
]
. In this last case, however,
the relation between the values of the two Casimir
operators involves a rank-dependant shift
stemming from
a quantum mechanical
ordering issue.

4.4
Inhomogeneous orthogonal group

The coadjoint orbits of inhomogeneous orthogonal group
IO(p,N-p)
can be classified as follows.
Let
P_{a}
and
J_{ab}
the generators of the Lie algebra and
{\cal P}^{a}
and
{\cal J}^{ab}
their duals. A coadjoint vector is given by
\phi=\phi_{I\,a}\,{\cal P}^{a}+\phi_{H\,ab}\,{\cal J}^{ab}
.
If a coadjoint orbit has
\phi_{I\,a}=0
, then it reduces to that of the subgroup
O(p,N-p)
,
which, for
p=1
, might be interpreted as the dS group of one lower dimensions.
Therefore, we focus on the coadjoint orbits with non-vanishing
\phi_{I}
.
As we have seen below (
4.17
),
\phi_{H}
can be chosen in
the dual space of the little group algebra associated with
\phi_{I}
:

O(p-1,q)
,
IO(p-1,q-1)
and
O(p,q-1)
for
\phi_{I}^{2}>0
,
\phi_{I}^{2}=0
and
\phi_{I}^{2}<0
.
The classification of
\phi_{H}
simply follows that of the
coadjoint orbits of the corresponding little group algebra.

Let us apply the general method outlined previously to
IO(p,N-p)
for which
H
as the indefinite orthogonal group
O(p,N-p)
and
I
as the translation group,

\mathbb{R}^{N}
carrying a vector representation
of
H
.
The resulting action reads

\displaystyle S[x,\Sigma,A]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\left[\phi_{I}{}_{a}\,(\Sigma^{-1})^{a}{}_{b}\,{\rm d}x^{b}+\phi_{H}{}^{a}{}_{b}\,(\Sigma^{-1})^{b}{}_{c}\,{\rm d}\Sigma^{c}{}_{a}\right]

(4.32)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\int\left[\phi_{I}{}_{a}\,\Sigma_{b}{}^{a}\,{\rm d}x^{b}+\phi_{H}{}^{ab}\,\Sigma_{cb}\,{\rm d}\Sigma^{c}{}_{a}+A^{ab}(\Sigma_{cb}\,\Sigma^{c}{}_{a}-\eta_{ba})\right]\,,

where we denote elements of the homogeneous group
H
by
\Sigma
,
and elements of the Abelian ideal
I
by
x
.
We first decompose the Lagrange multiplier as
A^{ab}=\phi_{I}{}^{a}\,\phi_{I}{}^{b}\,B+\phi_{I}{}^{(a}\,B^{b)}+B^{ab}
,
then skew-diagonalise and normalize
\phi_{H}
as
\phi_{H}{}^{ab}=T^{a}{}_{\alpha}\,T^{b}{}_{\beta}\,\Omega^{\alpha\beta}
.
Here again,
\alpha,\beta=1,\ldots,2M
where
2M
is the rank of
\phi_{H}
.
Finally,
by substituting

(B,B^{a},B^{ab})
with

(A,A^{a},A^{ab})
again,
and discarding
non-dynamical variables, we
reduce the action as

\displaystyle S[x,p,\Sigma,A]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\Big[\,p_{a}\,{\rm d}x^{a}+\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,\Sigma_{c\beta}\,{\rm d}\Sigma^{c}{}_{\alpha}+A\,(p_{a}\,p^{a}-\tilde{\phi}_{C})

(4.33)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.0pt+\,A^{\alpha}\,(p_{a}\,\Sigma^{a}{}_{\alpha}-\tilde{\phi}_{I\,\alpha})+A^{\alpha\beta}\,(\Sigma_{c\beta}\,\Sigma^{c}{}_{\alpha}-\tilde{\phi}_{H\,\beta\alpha})\Big]\,,

where
p_{a}=\Sigma_{a}{}^{b}\,\phi_{I}{}_{b}

and

\tilde{\phi}_{H\,\alpha\beta}=T^{a}{}_{\alpha}\,T^{b}{}_{\beta}\,\eta_{ab}\,,\quad\tilde{\phi}_{I\,\alpha}=\phi_{I\,a}\,T^{a}{}_{\alpha},\quad\tilde{\phi}_{C}=\phi_{I\,a}\,\phi_{I}{}^{a}\,.

(4.34)

Here, the dual algebra is associated with the moment maps

\mu_{\alpha\beta}=\Sigma_{c\alpha}\,\Sigma^{c}{}_{\beta}
,

\mu_{\alpha}=p_{a}\,\Sigma^{a}{}_{\alpha}
and
\mu=p_{a}\,p^{a}

satisfying (
4.27
) and

\{\mu_{\alpha\beta},\mu_{\gamma}\}=2\,\mu_{(\alpha}\,\Omega_{\beta)\gamma}\,,\qquad\{\mu_{\alpha},\mu_{\beta}\}=\Omega_{\alpha\beta}\,\mu\,,

(4.35)

where
\mu
is the center. This Lie algebra
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{heis}_{2M}\niplus\mathfrak{sp}(2M,\mathbb{R})

with dimension
(M+1)(2M+1)
,
the semi-direct sum of the Heisenberg
and the symplectic algebra. To recapitulate,

\phi_{H}=T\,\Omega\,T^{t}\,\eta\,,\qquad\tilde{\phi}_{H}=\Omega\,T^{t}\,\eta\,T\,,\qquad\phi_{I}\,T=\tilde{\phi}_{I}\,,

(4.36)

and we find that the following three quantities

{\rm Tr}(\phi_{H}{}^{n})=I_{n}={\rm Tr}(\tilde{\phi}_{H}{}^{n})\,,\qquad(\phi_{I}|\phi_{I})=J_{2}=\tilde{\phi}_{C}\,,

(4.37)

and

(\phi_{I}|\phi_{H}{}^{n}|\phi_{I}{})=J_{n+2}=\langle\tilde{\phi}_{I}|\tilde{\phi}_{H}{}^{n-1}|{\tilde{\phi}_{I}}\rangle\qquad[n\geq 1]\,,

(4.38)

where

(v|A|w)=v_{a}\,A^{a}{}_{b}\,\eta^{bc}\,w_{c}\,,\qquad\langle v|A|w\rangle=v_{\alpha}\,A^{\alpha}{}_{\beta}\,\Omega^{\beta\gamma}\,w_{\gamma}\,,

(4.39)

relating the same traces of powers
of
\phi=(\phi_{H},\phi_{I})
and its dual

\tilde{\phi}=(\tilde{\phi}_{H},\tilde{\phi}_{I})
.
However,
I_{n}
and
J_{n}
are invariant only
under the homogeneous part of the group,

G_{H}
or
\tilde{G}_{H}
, except for
J_{2}
.
The higher order Casimir invariant functions

C_{2(n+1)}
for the full Poincaré algebra
can be constructed using the Pauli–Lubanski
tensors
W_{(n+1)}
given by,

W_{(n+1)}{}^{a_{1}\cdots a_{d-2n-1}}=\dfrac{1}{2^{n}\,n!}\,\varepsilon^{a_{1}\ldots a_{d}}\,\phi_{H}\,{}_{a_{d-2n}a_{d-2n+1}}\,\cdots\,\phi_{H}\,{}_{a_{d-2}a_{d-1}}\,\phi_{I}\,{}_{a_{d}}\,,

(4.40)

as (see e.g.
[
144
]
)

C_{2(n+1)}=\frac{1}{(d-2n-1)!}\,W_{(n+1)}{}^{a_{1}\cdots a_{d-2n-1}}\,W_{(n+1)\,a_{1}\cdots a_{d-2n-1}}\,.

(4.41)

These invariant functions can be expressed
in terms of
I_{n}
and
J_{n}
,
and they are also invariant under the dual group.
For our purpose, it is sufficient to consider
the first two,

C_{2}=-J_{2}\,,\qquad C_{4}=-J_{4}+\frac{1}{2}\,I_{2}\,J_{2}\,.

(4.42)

5
Vectorial description of particles
in Minkowski space

In Section
4.4
,
we have already presented the derivation of covariant actions
from coadjoint orbits of inhomogeneous Lorentz groups.
Let us resume our analysis with the action (
4.33
),

\displaystyle S[x,p,\Sigma,A]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\Big[\,p_{a}\,{\rm d}\,x^{a}+\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,\Sigma_{c\beta}\,{\rm d}\,\Sigma^{c}{}_{\alpha}+A\,(p_{a}\,p^{a}-\tilde{\phi}_{C})

(5.1)

\displaystyle\hskip 45.0pt+\,A^{\alpha}\,(p_{a}\,\Sigma^{a}{}_{\alpha}-\tilde{\phi}_{I\,\alpha})+A^{\alpha\beta}\,(\Sigma_{c\beta}\,\Sigma^{c}{}_{\alpha}-\tilde{\phi}_{H\,\beta\alpha})\Big]\,,

where the vector indices
a
,
b
run from
0
to
d-1
,
and the spin-variable indices
\alpha,\beta
run from
1
to
2M
.
Recall that
2M
is the rank of
\phi_{H}
.
In case of the usual spinning particle,
M
corresponds essentially
to the number of rows of the Young diagram
of the (mixed-symmetry) tensor field
associated with the spinning particle under consideration.
The Hamiltonian constraints correspond to
a coadjoint orbit of the dual algebra

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{heis}_{2M}\niplus\mathfrak{sp}(2M,\mathbb{R})\,,

(5.2)

generated by

T=p^{2}\,,\qquad M_{\alpha}=p_{a}\,\Sigma^{a}{}_{\alpha}\,,\qquad S_{\alpha\beta}=\Sigma_{a\alpha}\,\Sigma^{a}{}_{\beta}\,.

(5.3)

The dual coadjoint vector
\tilde{\phi}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}

is given by

\tilde{\phi}=\tilde{\phi}_{C}\,{\cal T}+\tilde{\phi}_{I\,\alpha}\,{\cal M}^{\alpha}+{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\,\alpha\beta}\,{\cal S}^{\alpha\beta}\,.

(5.4)

We can integrate out
p_{a}
from the Hamiltonian type
action (
5.1
) to get a Polyakov-type
Lagrangian action,

L=-\frac{1}{2\,e}(\dot{x}^{a}+\lambda^{\alpha}\,\Sigma^{a}{}_{\alpha})^{2}-\lambda^{\alpha}\,\phi_{I\,\alpha}-\frac{e}{2}\,\phi_{I}{}^{2}+\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,\Sigma_{a\alpha}\,\dot{\Sigma}^{a}{}_{\beta}+\lambda^{\alpha\beta}\,(\Sigma_{a\beta}\,\Sigma^{a}{}_{\alpha}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\beta\alpha})\,,

(5.5)

with
A=\frac{e}{2}\,{\rm d}t
,
A^{\alpha}=\lambda^{\alpha}\,{\rm d}t
and
A^{\alpha\beta}=\lambda^{\alpha\beta}\,{\rm d}t
.
If the matrix
\Sigma_{a\alpha}\,\Sigma^{a}{}_{\beta}\simeq{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\alpha\beta}

is invertible with the inverse
\Delta^{\alpha\beta}
,
we can also remove
\lambda^{\alpha}
in terms of its equation of motion to get

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\,e}\left(\dot{x}^{a}{}^{2}-\dot{x}^{a}\,\Sigma_{a\alpha}\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,\Sigma_{b\beta}\,\dot{x}^{b}\right)-\frac{e}{2}\left(\tilde{\phi}_{C}-\tilde{\phi}_{I\alpha}\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,\tilde{\phi}_{I\beta}\right)

(5.6)

\displaystyle+\,\Sigma_{a\beta}\left(\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,\dot{\Sigma}^{a}{}_{\alpha}+\dot{x}^{a}\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,\tilde{\phi}_{I\alpha}\right)+\lambda^{\alpha\beta}\,(\Sigma_{a\beta}\,\Sigma^{a}{}_{\alpha}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\beta\alpha})\,.

In the case where
\lambda^{\alpha}
cannot be solved,
it is associated with a first class constraint.
Finally, when
\tilde{\phi}_{C}-\tilde{\phi}_{I\alpha}\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,\tilde{\phi}_{I\beta}\neq 0
,
we can solve
e
out to find the Nambu-type action,

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sqrt{\left(\tilde{\phi}_{C}-\tilde{\phi}_{I\alpha}\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,\tilde{\phi}_{I\beta}\right)\left(\dot{x}^{a}{}^{2}-\dot{x}^{a}\,\Sigma_{a\alpha}\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,\Sigma_{b\beta}\,\dot{x}^{b}\right)}

(5.7)

\displaystyle+\,\Sigma_{a\beta}\left(\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,\dot{\Sigma}^{a}{}_{\alpha}+\dot{x}^{a}\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,\tilde{\phi}_{I\alpha}\right)+\lambda^{\alpha\beta}\,(\Sigma_{a\beta}\,\Sigma^{a}{}_{\alpha}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\beta\alpha})\,.

Even though the above action contains all the parameters
\tilde{\phi}_{C},\tilde{\phi}_{I\alpha}
and
{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\alpha\beta}
,
the dependence on
\tilde{\phi}_{I\alpha}
is in fact irrelevant because
\tilde{\phi}_{I\alpha}
are non-trivial
only when
\Sigma_{a\alpha}\,\Sigma^{a}{}_{\beta}
is not invertible. The above type of the action
has been derived in
[
51
]
for
massive spinning particles.
One may even convert the spin variables into
Lagrangian
[
49
,
50
]

ending up with a double square root type action.

Below, we present more details of the scalar particles
(
M=0
) and the spinning particles (
M=1
),
along with the classification
of coadjoint orbits of the Poincaré group.
See
[
145
,
146
]

for explicit characterisations of the
Poincaré group orbits.
See also
[
147
]

for a discussion of the coadjoint orbits of the Carroll group.

5.1
Scalar particles

In the scalar particle case, we have
\phi_{H\,ab}=0
,
that is
{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\,\alpha\beta}=0
,
and the action gets simplified to the familiar form,

S[x,p,A]=\int p_{a}\,{\rm d}x^{a}+A\,(p_{a}\,p^{a}-\tilde{\phi}_{C})\,.

(5.8)

The dual algebra in this case is merely

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathbb{R}
, generated by
T=p^{2}
.
The dual coadjoint orbit is given by
{\tilde{\phi}}=\tilde{\phi}_{C}\,{\cal T}

and the stabiliser is the dual algebra itself

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathbb{R}
.
Depending on the signature of the vector
\tilde{\phi}_{I\,a}
,
we have massive, massless and tachyonic particles
with mass squared given by
\phi_{I}{}^{2}
.
The corresponding stabilisers are

\mathfrak{iso}(1,d-1)_{m\,{\cal P}^{0}}=\mathbb{R}_{P_{0}}\oplus\mathfrak{so}(d-1)_{[1,\dots,d-1]}\,,

(5.9a)

\mathfrak{iso}(1,d-1)_{E\,{\cal P}^{+}}=\mathbb{R}_{P_{-}}\oplus\mathfrak{iso}(d-2)_{[-;1,\dots,d-2]}\,,

(5.9b)

\mathfrak{iso}(1,d-1)_{\mu\,{\cal P}^{d-1}}=\mathbb{R}_{P_{d-1}}\oplus\mathfrak{so}(1,d-2)_{[0,1,\dots,d-2]}\,,

(5.9c)

where the subscripts refer to a basis for the stabilisers
of the representative considered here,
see Appendix
A
for details on this notation.
The dimensions of these coadjoint orbits are all
2(d-1)

implying that they describe
d
-dimensional particles.
Indeed, the Hilbert space corresponding to these particles
will consist of wave functions on a
(d-1)
-dimensional Cauchy surface, which is a Lagrangian submanifold of the phase space.
The dual coadjoint orbits are all zero-dimensional as each of them is a single point.
Remark that the massless coadjoint orbit is nilpotent
and it is dual to the trivial orbit.
In the dimension counting (
3.19
),
we have
\dim\,{\cal M}=2d
(here
{\cal M}=T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{d}
),
\dim\,{{\cal O}}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=0
and
\dim\,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=1
,
and hence
\dim\,{\cal N}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\dim\,{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}=2(d-1)
.

5.2
Spinning particles

In the spinning particle case with
M=1
,
we relabel once again
(\Sigma^{a}{}_{\chi},\Sigma^{a}{}_{\pi})
,
the two non-trivial vectors in
\Sigma^{a}{}_{\alpha}
, as
(\chi^{a},\pi^{a})

(note here we use
\chi,\pi
to denote both the indices
and the vectors). The resulting action reads

\displaystyle S[x,p,\chi,\pi,A]=\int\Big[\,p\cdot{\rm d}x+\pi\cdot{\rm d}\chi+A\,(p^{2}-\tilde{\phi}_{C})+A^{\chi}\,(p\cdot\chi-\tilde{\phi}_{I\chi})+A^{\pi}\,(p\cdot\pi-\tilde{\phi}_{I\pi})

\displaystyle\hskip 45.0pt+\,A^{\chi\chi}\,(\chi^{2}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\chi\chi})+A^{\chi\pi}\,(\chi\cdot\pi-{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\chi\pi})+A^{\pi\pi}\,(\pi^{2}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\pi\pi})\Big]\,,

(5.10)

where we used the shorthand notation

v\cdot w=v^{a}\,w_{a}
for contraction of Lorentz indices.
When this particle action is quantized,
a part
17
17

17

In case of second class constraints, only half of the constraints can be consistently imposed at the quantum level.

of the constraints will be associated with the field equations:
with
p_{\mu}\,\sim\,\partial/\partial x^{\mu}\ \text{and}\ \pi_{\mu}\,\sim\,\partial/\partial\chi^{\mu}
,
the terms
p^{2},p\cdot\chi,p\cdot\pi,\chi\cdot\pi\ \text{and}\ \pi^{2}
will be mapped to the
d’Alembertian, gradient, divergence, spin number and trace operators respectively.

18
18

18

In fact, it seems reasonable to expect that such an interpretation holds,
in some sense, for the three types of constraints appearing in the general action (
5.1
):
the first one is generically a mass-shell constraint, the second one correspond to a type of transversality condition,
and the third one to symmetry and trace condition,
to be imposed on the tensor field describing the particle.

More details of this association will be treated in our forthcoming paper.

Below, we shall provide the classification
of the coadjoint orbits
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}
of Poincaré
algebra with
M=1
and the corresponding coadjoint orbit

{\cal O}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}
of the dual algebra

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{heis}_{2}\niplus\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
Note that we will always assume that the labels
of the representative vectors are generic:
they are non-vanishing and different unless stated otherwise.
From the vector,

{\tilde{\phi}}=\tilde{\phi}_{C}\ {\cal T}+\tilde{\phi}_{I\,\chi}\,{\cal M}^{\chi}+\tilde{\phi}_{I\,\pi}\,{\cal M}^{\pi}+{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\,\chi\chi}\,{\cal S}^{\chi\chi}+2\,{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\,\chi\pi}\,{\cal S}^{\chi\pi}+{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\,\pi\pi}\,{\cal S}^{\pi\pi}\,,

(5.11)

one can read off the parameters which appear in the particle action.
The dimensions of
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}
is

\dim\mathfrak{iso}(1,d-1)-\dim\,\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\frac{d(d+1)}{2}-\dim\,\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}\,,

whereas in the dimension counting (
3.19
) we find

\displaystyle{\rm dim}\,{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}={\rm dim}\,{\cal N}_{\tilde{\phi}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm dim}\,{\cal M}-{\rm dim}\,{{\cal O}}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}-2\,{\rm dim}\,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}

(5.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm dim}\,{\cal M}-{\rm dim}\,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}-{\rm dim}\,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=2(2d-3)-{\rm dim}\,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}\,.

As we shall see below,
\dim\,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}

is either
2
or
4
in the
M=1
case. Therefore,
the corresponding phase spaces have dimensions
either
2(2d-4)
or
2(2d-5)
.
Comparing these with the phase space dimensions
of scalar particles,
2(d-1)
,
they are greater by
2(d-3)
or
2(d-4)
.
As a mechanical system, one may interpret these dimensions
directly as the number of degrees of freedom
but here lies a subtlety. As we have discussed
with the example of the compact coadjoint orbit
S^{2}
of
SU(2)
,
a compact phase space does not contribute
to the continuous degrees of freedom but only discrete labels.
In the usual spinning particle case, the additional dimensions
can be understood as ‘spin-orbits’,
which often correspond to the compact part
of the fiber of the coadjoint orbit viewed as
a fiber bundle over the momentum orbit
[
139
,
140
]
,
contributing again only some discrete labels.

In the following, we present the classification
of coadjoint orbits with
M=1
.

•

A massive spinning particle corresponds
to the coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}

with representative and stabiliser given by

\phi=m\,{\cal P}^{0}+s\,{\cal J}^{12}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathbb{R}_{P_{0}}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)_{J_{12}}\oplus\mathfrak{so}(d-3)_{[3,\dots,d-1]}\,.

(5.13)

The coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}\simeq\frac{IO(1,d-1)}{\mathbb{R}\times O(2)\times O(d-3)}

can be viewed as a fiber bundle
with the massive scalar coadjoint orbit

\frac{IO(1,d-1)}{{\mathbb{R}}\times O(d-1)}

(which is the cotangent bundle of the momentum orbit)
as the base space and the spin-orbit

\frac{O(d-1)}{O(2)\times O(d-3)}
as the fiber.
The latter, the real Grassmannian
{\rm Gr}_{\mathbb{R}}(2,d-1)
,
is a compact manifold, and hence contributes only
to discrete degrees of freedom, when quantised.
With the choice of the indices
\chi=1,\pi=2
,
the dual coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}

is characterised by

\tilde{\phi}=-m^{2}\,{\cal T}+{\cal S}^{\chi\chi}+s^{2}\,{\cal S}^{\pi\pi}\,,\qquad\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\mathbb{R}_{T}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)_{S_{\pi\pi}+s^{2}\,S_{\chi\chi}}\,.

(5.14)

The quadratic and quartic Casimir functions
of this orbit are given by

C_{2}=m^{2}\,,\qquad C_{4}=m^{2}\,s^{2}\,,

(5.15)

and, up to a shift (that should originate
from an ordering issue when quantising) reproduces
the value of the Casimir operators of the Poincaré
group on the irrep corresponding to a massive spinning
particle. See
[
51
]

for the derivation of a related worldline action
for a massive spinning particle in flat spacetime.

•

A massless spinning particle corresponds to the coadjoint
orbit
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}
with representative and stabiliser

\phi=E\,{\cal P}^{+}+s\,{\cal J}^{12}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\big(\mathfrak{heis}_{2}\niplus\mathfrak{u}(1)_{J_{12}}\big)\oplus\mathfrak{iso}(d-4)_{[-;3,\dots,d-2]}\,,

(5.16)

and where the Heisenberg algebra is generated by

-E\,J_{-2}+\,s\,P_{1},\qquad E\,J_{-1}+\,s\,P_{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad P_{-}\,.

(5.17)

With the indices
\chi=1,\pi=2
, we find the dual
coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}
,
characterised by

\tilde{\phi}={\cal S}^{\chi\chi}+s^{2}\,{\cal S}^{\pi\pi}\,,\qquad\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\mathfrak{heis}_{2}\niplus\mathfrak{u}(1)\,.

(5.18)

with stabiliser generated by
T
,
M_{\pi}
,
M_{\chi}
,
and
S_{\pi\pi}+s^{2}\,S_{\chi\chi}
.
Note that
E
is not a proper label for the orbit
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}

because rescaling of
E
does not change the orbit.
We can verify this in the dual coadjoint orbit

{\cal O}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}
: the coadjoint vector
\tilde{\phi}

does not depend on
E
. The Casimir functions
of this orbit vanish:
C_{2}=0=C_{4}
.

•

A continuous spinning particle corresponds
to the coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}
with representative
and stabiliser

\phi=E\,{\cal P}^{+}+\epsilon\,{\cal J}^{-1}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathbb{R}_{P_{-}}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{2\,\epsilon\,P_{+}+EJ_{-1}}\oplus\mathfrak{so}(d-3)_{[2,\dots,d-2]}\,.

(5.19)

With the indices
\chi=1,\pi=-
, we find the dual coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}
is characterised by

\tilde{\phi}=-E\,\epsilon\,{\cal M}^{\pi}+\,{\cal S}^{\chi\chi}\,,\qquad\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\mathbb{R}_{T}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{2\,M_{\pi}-E\,\epsilon\,S_{\chi\chi}}\,.

(5.20)

Here again
E
and
\epsilon
are not proper labels for
{\cal O}_{\phi}^{G}
,
but only the combination
\varepsilon^{2}=E\,\epsilon
is
(note that the sign of
{\cal M}^{\pi}
term
can be changed by a coadjoint action, and that
the square symbol should not be understood literally,
i.e.
\varepsilon^{2}
may be either positive or negative).
Hence, the particle action involves only one parameter,

\varepsilon
. The Casimir functions of this orbit
take the values

C_{2}=0\,,\qquad C_{4}=4\,\varepsilon^{4}.

(5.21)

Remark that the massive and massless spinning particles
share the same spin part
s\,{\cal J}^{12}

and
{\cal S}^{\chi\chi}+s^{2}{\cal S}^{\pi\pi}

in the coadjoint vectors
\phi
and
\tilde{\phi}
.
We may refer to this as space-like spin.
In the continuous spin case, the spin part of the coadjoint orbits
are
\epsilon\,{\cal J}^{-1}
and
{\cal S}^{\chi\chi}
are null-vectors,
so we may refer this as light-like spin.
Note that a particle action for continuous spin
fields was discussed in
[
148
,
149
,
41
]
,
which involves
4 first class constraints
corresponding to
Wigner’s equations
[
150
]
,
whereas
our system involves
2 first and 4 second class constraints,
which can be viewed
as a partially gauge fixed version of the former.
See e.g.
[
151
,
152
,
153
,
154
,
155
]

for related works.

•

There are three sub-categories for a tachyonic spinning particle.
The first case is the space-like spin coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}

with representative and stabiliser given by

\phi=\mu\,{\cal P}^{d-1}+s\,{\cal J}^{12}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathbb{R}_{P_{d-1}}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)_{J_{12}}\oplus\mathfrak{so}(1,d-4)_{[0,3,\dots,d-2]}\,.

(5.22)

With the indices
\chi=1,\pi=2
, we find the dual coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}
is characterised by

\tilde{\phi}=\mu^{2}\,{\cal T}+{\cal S}^{\chi\chi}+s^{2}\,{\cal S}^{\pi\pi}\,,\qquad\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\mathbb{R}_{T}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)_{S_{\pi\pi}+s^{2}\,S_{\chi\chi}}\,.

(5.23)

Here, one can also note that the spin part shares
the same structure as the massive and massless spinning case.
The Casimir functions of this orbits are

C_{2}=-\mu^{2}\,,\qquad C_{4}=-\mu^{2}s^{2}.

(5.24)

and are related to those of a massive spinning
orbit (
5.13
) by setting
m=i\mu
,
in accordance with our interpretation as a tachyonic
spinning orbit.

•

The second case is the time-like spin coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}
,
with representative and stabiliser

\phi=\mu\,{\cal P}^{d-1}+\nu\,{\cal J}^{01}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathbb{R}_{P_{d-1}}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{J_{02}}\oplus\mathfrak{so}(d-3)_{[2,\dots,d-2]}\,.

(5.25)

With the indices
\chi=0,\pi=1
, we find the dual coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}
is characterised by

\tilde{\phi}=\mu^{2}\,{\cal T}+{\cal S}^{\chi\chi}-\nu^{2}{\cal S}^{\pi\pi}\,,\qquad\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\mathbb{R}_{T}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{S_{\pi\pi}-\nu^{2}\,S_{\chi\chi}}\,.

(5.26)

The Casimir functions of this orbit are given by

C_{2}=-\mu^{2}\,,\qquad C_{4}=\mu^{2}\nu^{2}\,,

(5.27)

and one can notice that they are related to
those of the tachyonic spinning orbit
with space-like (
5.24
)
by setting
s=i\,\nu
.

•

The last case is the light-like spin
coadjoint orbit
{\cal O}^{G}_{\phi}
with representative
and stabiliser

\phi=\mu\,{\cal P}^{d-1}+\epsilon\,{\cal J}^{-2}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathbb{R}_{P_{d-1}}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{J_{+2}}\oplus\mathfrak{iso}(d-4)_{[+;3,\dots,d-2]}\,.

(5.28)

With the indices
\chi=2,\pi=-
, we find that the dual
coadjoint orbit
\tilde{\cal O}^{\tilde{G}}_{\tilde{\phi}}

is characterised by

\tilde{\phi}=\mu^{2}\,{\cal T}+\,{\cal S}^{\chi\chi}\,,\qquad\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\mathbb{R}_{T}\oplus\mathbb{R}_{S_{\pi\pi}}\,.

(5.29)

The Casimir functions of this orbit read

C_{2}=-\mu^{2}\,,\qquad C_{4}=0\,.

(5.30)

Remark that except for the continuous spin particles,
all other particles are described by the action
with two parameters
\mathcal{C}_{M}
and
\mathcal{C}_{S}

in the end:

\displaystyle S[x,p,\chi,\pi,A]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\Big[\,p_{a}\,{\rm d}\,x^{a}+\pi_{a}\,{\rm d}\,\chi^{a}+A\,(p^{2}+\mathcal{C}_{M})+A^{\chi}\,p\cdot\chi+A^{\pi}\,p\cdot\pi

(5.31)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.0pt+\,A^{\chi\chi}\,(\chi^{2}-1)+A^{\chi\pi}\,\chi\cdot\pi+A^{\pi\pi}\,(\pi^{2}-\,\mathcal{C}_{S})\Big]\,.

Massive, massless and tachyonic particles of spin
s

are described by
\mathcal{C}_{S}=s^{2}
and positive,
zero and negative values of
\mathcal{C}_{M}
, respectively.
The tachyonic particles of time-like and light-like spins
are described by a negative and zero
\mathcal{C}_{S}
,
respectively, and a negative
\mathcal{C}_{M}
.

Let us also recapitulate the system of constraints
of the above particle action.
Each of constraints are associated with the generators of the dual algebra as

\begin{gathered}\chi^{*}(T)=p^{2}+\mathcal{C}_{M}\,,\qquad\chi^{*}(M_{\chi})=p\cdot\chi\,,\qquad\chi^{*}(M_{\pi})=p\cdot\pi\,,\\
\chi^{*}(S_{\chi\chi})=\chi^{2}-1\,,\qquad\chi^{*}(S_{\chi\pi})=\chi\cdot\pi\,,\qquad\chi^{*}(S_{\pi\pi})=\pi^{2}-\mathcal{C}_{S}\,,\end{gathered}

(5.32)

and they are either first or second class constraints depending on the value of
\mathcal{C}_{M}
and
\mathcal{C}_{S}
.
For the massive and tachyonic cases with
\mathcal{C}_{M}\neq 0
,
we have two first class constraints,

\chi^{*}(T)\,,\qquad\chi^{*}(S_{\pi\pi}+\mathcal{C}_{S}\,S_{\chi\chi})\,,

(5.33)

forming the Lie algebra
\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
,
while the other
4
constraints are second class.
For the massless case with
\mathcal{C}_{M}=0
, we have four first class constraints,

\chi^{*}(T)\,,\qquad\chi^{*}(S_{\pi\pi}+\mathcal{C}_{S}\,S_{\chi\chi})\,,\qquad\chi^{*}(M_{\chi})\,,\qquad\chi^{*}(M_{\pi})\,,

(5.34)

forming the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{heis}_{2}\niplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
,
while the other
2
constraints are second class.

5.3
Spinning particles with mixed symmetry

The coadjoint orbits with higher
M
correspond typically
to spinning particles with mixed symmetry, characterised
by a
M
-row Young diagram. In the following, we shall provide
the representative vectors
\phi
of the coadjoint orbits
with higher
M
and their stabilisers
\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}
.
The stabilisers of the dual algebra

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}
are always isomorphic
to the
d
-independent part of
\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}
.

The coadjoint orbit of a mixed symmetry spinning particle is described by a representative vector
where the space-like spin
s\,{\cal J}^{12}
is replaced by

s_{1}\,{\cal J}^{12}+s_{2}\,{\cal J}^{34}+\cdots+s_{M}\,{\cal J}^{2M-1\,2M}\,,\qquad M\leq[\tfrac{d}{2}]\,,

(5.35)

where
[x]
denotes the integer part of
x
,
and where we can also assume that

s_{1}\geq s_{2}\geq\dots\geq s_{M}\,,

(5.36)

without loss of generality. If
s_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
,
then this defines a Young diagram. In order to take
into account the possibility that several consecutive
rows of the diagram have the same length, i.e.

\ell_{1}:=s_{1}=\cdots=s_{h_{1}}\,,\qquad\ell_{2}:=s_{h_{1}+1}=\cdots=s_{h_{1}+h_{2}}\,,

(5.37)

and so on, it is convenient to describe
the diagram in terms of blocks of width
\ell_{k}

and height
h_{k}
, as illustrated in Figure
5
below.

\leftarrow\hskip 40.0pt\ell_{1}\hskip 40.0pt\rightarrow
\uparrow
h_{1}
\downarrow
\leftarrow\qquad\ell_{2}\qquad\rightarrow
\uparrow
h_{2}
\downarrow
\leftarrow\ \ell_{p}\ \rightarrow
\uparrow
h_{p}
\downarrow

Figure 5
:
Young diagram presented in block form.

The stabilisers of such spinning particles are given by

\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(h_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus\mathfrak{u}(h_{p})\oplus\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathfrak{so}(d-1-2M)\qquad&\quad\text{[massive],}\\
\mathfrak{so}(1,d-2-2M)\quad&\quad\text{[tachyonic],}\end{aligned}\right.

(5.38)

and in the massless case,

\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathfrak{heis}_{2M}\niplus\Big(\mathfrak{u}(h_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus\mathfrak{u}(h_{p})\Big)\oplus\mathfrak{iso}(d-2-2M)\,,

(5.39)

where
h_{1}+\dots+h_{p}=M
.

In the case of the coadjoint orbits
with light-like or time-like spin,
simply the
M-1
space-like spins
are added on top of the former.
We therefore find the stabilisers

\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(h_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus\mathfrak{u}(h_{p})\oplus\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathfrak{so}(d-1-2M)&\quad\text{[continuous spin}\\
&\qquad\text{and time-like tachyonic],}\\
\mathfrak{iso}(d-2-2M)&\quad\text{[light-like tachyonic],}\end{aligned}\right.

(5.40)

where
h_{1}+\dots+h_{p}=M-1
.

5.4
Null particles

The last class of particles with Poincaré symmetry
are what we refer to as ‘null’ particles,
corresponding to the coadjoint orbit with
\phi_{I}=0
.
Clearly, the condition
\phi_{I}=0

trivializes the ideal — translational — part of
the Poincaré algebra, and hence such orbits
are simply identical to Lorentz coadjoint orbits.
Upon quantisation, these coadjoint orbits
would correspond to the unfaithful representations
of Poincaré with
p_{\mu}=0
and the little group
SO(1,d-1)
,
hence again reduces to unitary irreducible representations
of Lorentz group. Since the Lorentz group can be viewed
as dS group of one lower dimensions, the classification
of null coadjoint orbits are the same as
the classification of dS coadjoint orbits.
The only differences are in the interpretation.
Even though the null coadjoint orbits
seem somewhat dull in their defining nature,
they may capture some important peculiarities
of Poincaré symmetry because analogous dull orbits
are not present for (A)dS symmetry. In fact,
the null particles can be interpreted as
the ‘soft limit’ of massless particles.
We shall come back to this point in
Section
7.2
.
Let us conclude this section by remarking that,
in a sense, these null particles can be viewed
as a kind of flat space analogue of the AdS singleton.
See
[
156
,
157
,
158
]

for more serious proposals concerning this issue.

6
Vectorial description of particles in (A)dS space

For the covariant description of various particles
in dS and AdS spaces,
we begin with the covariant action (
4.26
)
of the orthogonal groups
O(p,N-p)
:

O(1,d)
for dS and
O(2,d-1)
for AdS.
The indices
\textsc{A},\textsc{B}
take values
0,1,\ldots,d-1

and
\bullet
(
\bullet=d
for dS
and
\bullet=0^{\prime}
for AdS).
The metric
\eta_{\textsc{AB}}
is
{\rm diag}(-1,+1,\ldots,+1,\sigma)

where
\sigma=+1
for dS and
-1
for AdS.
Relabeling the variables as

X^{\textsc{A}}{}_{\bullet}=X^{\textsc{A}}\,,\qquad X^{\textsc{A}}{}_{\beta}\,\phi_{P}{}^{\beta}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,P^{\textsc{A}}\,,\qquad X^{\textsc{A}}{}_{\alpha}=\Sigma^{\textsc{A}}{}_{\alpha}\,,\qquad[\alpha\geq 3]\,,

(6.1)

the action can be expressed in a more familiar form,

\displaystyle S[X,P,\Sigma,A]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\Big[P\cdot{\rm d}X+\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,\Sigma_{\beta}\cdot{\rm d}\Sigma_{\alpha}

(6.2)

\displaystyle\quad+\,A^{XX}(X^{2}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{XX})+A^{PP}(P^{2}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{PP})+A^{XP}(X\cdot P-{\tilde{\phi}}_{XP})

\displaystyle\quad+\,A^{X\alpha}(X\cdot\Sigma_{\alpha}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{X\alpha})+A^{P\alpha}(P\cdot\Sigma_{\alpha}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{P\alpha})+A^{\alpha\beta}\big(\Sigma_{\beta}\cdot\Sigma_{\alpha}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{\alpha\beta}\big)\Big]\,,

where
X^{\textsc{A}}
and
P_{\textsc{A}}
will play the role
of the ambient space position and momentum.
Here again we used the notation

v\cdot w=v^{\textsc{A}}\,w_{\textsc{A}}
for contraction
of ambient indices. The Hamiltonian constraints
are associated with the dual algebra,
19
19

19

Note that
in this section, we use the convention
that the rank of the dual group is
M+1
,
different from the convention used
in Section
4.3

where the rank was
M
.

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sp}(2(M+1),\mathbb{R})\,,

(6.3)

generated by

T:=P^{2}\,,\quad U:=X^{2}\,,\quad V:=X\cdot P\,,

(6.4a)

M_{\alpha}:=P\cdot\Sigma_{\alpha}\,,\quad N_{\alpha}:=X\cdot\Sigma_{\alpha}\,,\quad S_{\alpha\beta}:=\Sigma_{\alpha}\cdot\Sigma_{\beta}\,.

(6.4b)

Note that the dimensions of
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
is
(M+1)(2M+3)
,
and differs from the dimension of the dual algebra of Poincaré

\mathfrak{heis}_{2M}\niplus\mathfrak{sp}(2M,\mathbb{R})
,
which is
(M+1)(2M+1)
, by
2(M+1)
.
20
20

20

Let us remark
that the dual of the Poincaré algebra appears as a subalgebra
of the Inönü–Wigner contraction
of
\mathfrak{sp}\big(2(M+1),\mathbb{R}\big)
that preserves
an
\mathfrak{sp}(2M,\mathbb{R})
subalgebra. More precisely,
this contraction yields a semi-direct sum

\mathfrak{sp}(2M,\mathbb{R})\inplus\mathfrak{n}_{2M}

where
\mathfrak{n}_{2M}
is a nilpotent Lie algebra,
made out of two copies of
\mathfrak{heis}_{2M}
and
a central term, and these two Heisenberg algebras
only commute with one another up to this central term.

This corresponds to the number of additional constraints necessary
to bring the ambient space to the intrinsic (A)dS.
In the following, the parameters in the action can be read off from
the dual coadjoint vector
{\tilde{\phi}}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}^{*}
,

{\tilde{\phi}}={\tilde{\phi}}_{PP}\,{\cal T}+{\tilde{\phi}}_{XX}\,{\cal U}+{\tilde{\phi}}_{XP}\,{\cal V}+{\tilde{\phi}}_{P\alpha}\,{\cal M}^{\alpha}+{\tilde{\phi}}_{X\alpha}\,{\cal N}^{\alpha}+{\tilde{\phi}}_{\alpha\beta}\,{\cal S}^{\alpha\beta}\,.

(6.5)

Similarly to the Minkowski case, we can integrate out
P_{\textsc{A}}

from the Hamiltonian type action (
6.2
)
to get a Polyakov-type Lagrangian action,

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\,e}\,({\cal D}_{t}\,X^{\textsc{A}}+\lambda^{\alpha}\,\Sigma^{\textsc{A}}{}_{\alpha})^{2}-\lambda^{\alpha}\,{\tilde{\phi}}_{P\alpha}-\frac{e}{2}\,{\tilde{\phi}}_{PP}+\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,\Sigma_{\alpha}\cdot\dot{\Sigma}_{\beta}

(6.6)

\displaystyle+\,\lambda^{\alpha\beta}\,(\Sigma_{\alpha}\cdot\Sigma_{\beta}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{H\beta\alpha})+\rho\,(X^{2}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{XX})-\tau\,{\tilde{\phi}}_{XP}+\tau^{\alpha}\,(X\cdot\Sigma_{\alpha}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{X\alpha})\,,

where
{\cal D}_{t}\,X^{\textsc{A}}=\dot{X}^{\textsc{A}}+\tau\,X^{\textsc{A}}

and the components of the gauge fields are

A^{XX}=\rho\,{\rm d}t
,

A^{PP}=\frac{e}{2}\,{\rm d}t
,

A^{XP}=\tau\,{\rm d}t
,

A^{X\alpha}=\tau^{\alpha}\,{\rm d}t
,

A^{P\alpha}=\lambda^{\alpha}\,{\rm d}t

and

A^{\alpha\beta}=\lambda^{\alpha\beta}\,{\rm d}t
.
As we shall see, we can always choose
a representative vector with
{\tilde{\phi}}_{XP}=0
.
In such a case, the equation for
\tau
simply
reduces to
\tau\,X^{2}+X\cdot\dot{X}+\lambda^{\alpha}\,\Sigma_{\alpha}\cdot X=0
.
If
\Sigma_{\alpha}\cdot X\simeq{\tilde{\phi}}_{X\alpha}=0
and
X^{2}\simeq{\tilde{\phi}}_{XX}\neq 0
,
we can remove
\tau
to get

{\cal D}_{t}X^{\textsc{A}}=\bar{\cal D}_{t}X^{\textsc{A}}=\dot{X}^{\textsc{A}}-\frac{X\cdot\dot{X}}{X^{2}}\,X^{\textsc{A}}\,.

(6.7)

Note that this expression is the pullback to the worldline
of the ambient lift of an (A)dS covariant derivative.
If the matrix

\Sigma_{\alpha}\cdot\Sigma_{\beta}\simeq{\tilde{\phi}}_{\alpha\beta}

is invertible with the inverse
\Delta^{\alpha\beta}
,
we can remove
\lambda^{\alpha}
to get

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\,e}\left(\bar{\cal D}_{t}X^{2}-\bar{\cal D}_{t}X\cdot\Sigma_{\alpha}\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,\Sigma_{\beta}\cdot\bar{\cal D}_{t}X\right)-\frac{e}{2}\left({\tilde{\phi}}_{PP}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{P\alpha}\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,{\tilde{\phi}}_{P\beta}\right)

(6.8)

\displaystyle+\,\Sigma_{\beta}\cdot\left(\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,\dot{\Sigma}_{\alpha}+\bar{\cal D}_{t}X\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,{\tilde{\phi}}_{P\beta}\right)+\lambda^{\alpha\beta}\,(\Sigma_{\alpha}\cdot\Sigma_{\beta}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{\beta\alpha})

\displaystyle+\,\rho\,(X^{2}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{XX})+\tau^{\alpha}\,X\cdot\Sigma_{\alpha}\,,

and for
{\tilde{\phi}}_{PP}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{P\alpha}\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,{\tilde{\phi}}_{P\beta}\neq 0
,
we can further remove
e
to get the Nambu-type action,

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sqrt{\left({\tilde{\phi}}_{PP}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{P\alpha}\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,{\tilde{\phi}}_{P\beta}\right)\left(\bar{\cal D}_{t}X^{2}-\bar{\cal D}_{t}X\cdot\Sigma_{\alpha}\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,\Sigma_{\beta}\cdot\bar{\cal D}_{t}X\right)}

(6.9)

\displaystyle+\,\Sigma_{\beta}\cdot\left(\Omega^{\alpha\beta}\,\dot{\Sigma}_{\alpha}+\bar{\cal D}_{t}X\,\Delta^{\alpha\beta}\,\phi_{I\beta}\right)+\lambda^{\alpha\beta}\,(\Sigma_{\alpha}\cdot\Sigma_{\beta}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{\beta\alpha})

\displaystyle+\,\rho\,(X^{2}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{XX})+\tau^{\alpha}\,X\cdot\Sigma_{\alpha}\,.

For the usual spinning particle type,
a similar type of action
has been derived in
[
26
,
50
]
.

In the following, we present the coadjoint vectors
of (A)dS algebra using a basis which singles out the Lorentz subalgebra
and with remaining,
transvection
, generators
defined as

P_{a}=\tfrac{1}{\ell}\,J_{\bullet a}\,,\qquad[P_{a},P_{b}]=\tfrac{\sigma}{\ell^{2}}\,J_{ab}\,,\qquad[a,b=0,1,\dots,d-1]\,,

(6.10)

where
\ell
is the (A)dS radius and its dual

{\cal P}^{a}=\ell\,{\cal J}^{\bullet a}
, in order to make
the analogy with the Minkowski case manifest.
From now on, we set
\ell=1
for simplicity.
As we shall see below, many cases can be viewed
as the (A)dS counterparts of the Poincaré orbits,
but there are also several cases which

do not
have a Poincaré analogue.
For the purpose of comparison between them,
it will be convenient to parameterise the Casimir
functions in terms of
I_{2}
and
I_{4}

(defined in eq. (
4.31
) previously) as

C_{2}=\sigma\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,I_{2}\,,\qquad C_{4}=\tfrac{1}{8}\,(I_{2})^{2}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,I_{4}\,.

(6.11)

This will also be useful to compare with
the results in literature.
For explicit characterisation of some (A)dS
coadjoint orbits, see e.g.

[
159
,
131
,
132
]
.

6.1
Scalar particles

In the scalar particle case,
we can always set
{\tilde{\phi}}_{XX}=\sigma

and
{\tilde{\phi}}_{XP}=0
, so that
the action depends only on
{\tilde{\phi}}_{PP}
:

S[X,P,A]=\int\Big[P\cdot{\rm d}X+A^{XX}(X^{2}-\sigma)+A^{PP}(P^{2}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{PP})+A^{XP}\,X\cdot P\Big]\,,\

(6.12)

where the Hamiltonian constraints are associated with the dual algebra
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
From the constraint
X^{2}=\sigma
, we can naturally interpret
X^{\textsc{A}}
as the ambient space coordinate for (A)dS spacetime. The condition
X\cdot P=0
can be understood as a fixed homogeneity,
and finally
P^{2}={\tilde{\phi}}_{PP}
is the mass-shell constraint.
See e.g.
[
160
]
for related analysis and discussions.
The coadjoint orbits of the dual
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathfrak{so}(2,1)

are given by the two-dimensional surfaces

H^{2}(\sigma\,{\tilde{\phi}}_{PP})
.
For more details, let us introduce a
\sigma
-dependent
notation for the one-dimensional Lie group
I(\sigma)
,

I(+1)=U(1)\,,\qquad I(-1)=\mathbb{R}\,,

(6.13)

and the associated Lie algebra
\mathfrak{i}(\sigma)
:

\mathfrak{i}(+1)=\mathfrak{u}(1)
and
\mathfrak{i}(-1)=\mathbb{R}
.
In the following, we match each one of the three types
of (A)dS scalar orbits — massive, massless and tachyonic —
with one of the three types of
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})\simeq\mathfrak{so}(2,1)

orbits
H^{2}(a)
defined in (
2.46
).

•

The (A)dS orbit of the massive particle is
given by

\phi=m\,{\cal P}^{0}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathfrak{i}(-\sigma)_{P_{0}}\oplus\mathfrak{so}(d-1)_{[1,\dots,d-1]}\,,

(6.14)

while the dual
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
orbit is given by

\tilde{\phi}=-m^{2}\,{\cal T}+\sigma\,{\cal U}\,,\qquad\tilde{G}_{\tilde{\phi}}=I(-\sigma)\,,

(6.15)

has the geometry of hyperboloid
H^{2}(-\sigma\,m^{2})
.

•

The massless particle orbit has representative and stabiliser,

\phi=E\,{\cal P}^{+}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathbb{R}_{P_{-}}\oplus\mathfrak{iso}(d-2)_{[-;1,\dots,d-2]}\,.

(6.16)

The dual orbit is characterised by

\tilde{\phi}=\sigma\,{\cal U}\,,\qquad\tilde{G}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\mathbb{R}

(6.17)

and corresponds to the cone
C^{2}
.

•

Lastly, the tachyonic particle orbit is given by

\phi=\mu\,{\cal P}^{1}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathfrak{i}(\sigma)_{P_{1}}\oplus\mathfrak{so}(1,d-2)_{[0,2,\dots,d-1]}\,.

(6.18)

The dual orbit has

\tilde{\phi}=\mu^{2}\,{\cal T}+\sigma\,{\cal U}\,,\qquad\tilde{G}_{\tilde{\phi}}=I(\sigma)\,,

(6.19)

and corresponds to the hyperboloid
H^{2}(\sigma\,\mu^{2})
.

Remark that the map of massive and tachyonic
coadjoint orbits of spacetime symmetry to the one-sheet
and two-sheet hyperbolic coadjoint orbits of
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})

works oppositely for AdS and dS.
Note that
C_{2}={\tilde{\phi}}_{XP}^{2}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{XX}\,{\tilde{\phi}}_{PP}

is a constant on these orbits. Massive scalar orbits in dS
and tachyonic scalar orbits in AdS
have positive
C_{2}
, and they can be given by
different representative vectors with
{\tilde{\phi}}_{PP}=0
:

({\tilde{\phi}}_{PP},{\tilde{\phi}}_{XX},{\tilde{\phi}}_{XP})=(0,1,m)
or
(0,-1,\mu)
.
Massive scalar orbits in AdS and tachyonic scalar orbits in dS
have negative
C_{2}
, and they do not contain
a vector with
{\tilde{\phi}}_{PP}=0
.
However, if we insist on it naively, they could be given by the complex vectors

({\tilde{\phi}}_{PP},{\tilde{\phi}}_{XX},{\tilde{\phi}}_{XP})=(0,-1,i\,m)
or
(0,1,i\,\mu)
.
This seemingly ill-defined choice of coadjoint vector
makes sense after quantisation: since
P_{\textsc{A}}=-i\,\hbar\,\partial/\partial X^{\textsc{A}}
,
it defines a homogeneity condition with a real degree of homogeneity and
hence corresponds to a more standard way to describe a AdS field using ambient space. The two choices are related by a
complexified global transformation — a
Sp(2,\mathbb{C})
rotation.

6.2
Spinning particles

Let us move to the spinning case with
M=1
.
Relabelling the non-trivial elements
(\Sigma^{\textsc{A}}{}_{\chi},\Sigma^{\textsc{A}}{}_{\pi})=(\chi^{\textsc{A}},\pi^{\textsc{A}})
,
we find

\displaystyle S[X,P,\chi,\pi,A]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\Big[P\cdot\mathrm{d}X+\pi\cdot\mathrm{d}\chi+A^{XX}(X^{2}-\sigma)+A^{PP}(P^{2}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{PP})+A^{XP}\,X\cdot P

(6.20)

\displaystyle\quad+\,A^{X\pi}(X\cdot\pi-{\tilde{\phi}}_{X\pi})+A^{X\chi}(X\cdot\chi-{\tilde{\phi}}_{X\chi})

\displaystyle\hskip 75.0pt+A^{P\pi}(P\cdot\pi-{\tilde{\phi}}_{P\pi})+A^{P\chi}(P\cdot\chi-{\tilde{\phi}}_{P\chi})

\displaystyle\quad+\,A^{\pi\pi}\big(\pi^{2}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{\pi\pi}\big)+A^{\chi\pi}\big(\chi\cdot\pi-{\tilde{\phi}}_{\chi\pi}\big)+A^{\chi\chi}\big(\chi^{2}-{\tilde{\phi}}_{\chi\chi}\big)\Big]\,,

where the Hamiltonian constraints are associated
with the dual algebra
\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
. Note that here

\chi^{\textsc{A}}
and
\pi_{\textsc{A}}
are
(d+1)
-dimensional vectors.
Comparing the dimension counting (
5.12
),
we find the same result as in the Minkowski case:

\dim\,{\cal O}_{\phi}=\dim\,{\cal M}-\dim\,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}-\dim\,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\phi}=2(2d-3)-\dim\,\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}\,,

(6.21)

where the increase of dimension in
{\cal M}
is compensated
by that of
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
. Compared to the Minkowski
particles and also to the (A)dS scalar particles,
the association of (A)dS coadjoint orbits with the spinning
particles in (A)dS is more subtle.
Therefore, we first provide the classification
using the simple terminologies that distinguish
the causal properties of the momenta and spins.

•

The coadjoint orbit with time-like momenta
and space-like spins has representative and stabiliser

\phi=m\,{\cal P}^{0}+s\,{\cal J}^{12}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathfrak{i}(-\sigma)_{P_{0}}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)_{J_{12}}\oplus\mathfrak{so}(d-3)_{[3,\dots,d-1]}\,.

(6.22)

Here, we take the non-trivial indices as
\chi=1,\pi=2
,
and find

{\tilde{\phi}}=-\,m^{2}\,{\cal T}+\sigma\,{\cal U}+{\cal S}^{\chi\chi}+s^{2}\,{\cal S}^{\pi\pi}\,,\qquad\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\mathfrak{i}(-\sigma)\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)\,,

(6.23)

where the stabiliser is generated by

T-\sigma\,m^{2}\,U
and
s^{2}\,S_{\chi\chi}+S_{\pi\pi}
.
The Casimir functions are given by

C_{2}=m^{2}-\sigma\,s^{2}\,,\qquad C_{4}=m^{2}s^{2}\,,

(6.24)

which, again up to a dimension-dependent shift,
agree with those of the
\mathfrak{so}(2,d-1)
-irreps
corresponding to massive spinning particles.
See
[
49
,
50
]

for the derivation of a related worldline action
of massive spinning particle in AdS. Note also
that the above set of constraints is the same
as the ones identified and used in the treatment
of massive and (partially-)massless mixed-symmetry
fields in AdS
d
using BRST techniques
and the ambient space approach

[
161
,
162
]
.

For dS, the mass parameter is a positive real
m>0
,
and all these orbits correspond
to massive spinning particles.

For AdS, the quantisation condition requires
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
and the value
m=s
is singular because
in that case we have different stabilisers,

\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathfrak{u}(1,1)\oplus\mathfrak{so}(d-3)_{[3,\dots,d-1]}\,,\qquad\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\mathfrak{u}(1,1)\,,

(6.25)

where the
\mathfrak{u}(1,1)
subalgebras are generated by

P_{1}-J_{02}\,,\qquad P_{2}+J_{01}\,,\qquad P_{0}+J_{12}\,,\qquad P_{0}-J_{12}\,,

(6.26)

and

T+s^{2}\,U\,,\qquad S_{\chi\chi}+s^{2}\,S_{\pi\pi}\,,\qquad M_{\chi}+\,N_{\pi}\,,\qquad M_{\pi}-s^{2}\,N_{\chi}\,,

(6.27)

respectively. Therefore, in AdS, we can interpret the case with
m>s

as massive spinning particles, and
m=s

as massless spinning particle.

One may expect that the coadjoint orbits with
m=s-1,s-2,\ldots,1

correspond to the partially massless representations in AdS,
with conformal weights
\Delta=s+d-4,s+d-5,\ldots,d-2
.
Up to the quantum shift,
\Delta=m+d-3
,
the labels of these representations seem to match those of the coadjoint orbits with
m=s-1,s-2,\ldots,1
.
However, these representations are not unitary
— they are unitary only in dS —
and one may conclude that this class of coadjoint orbits
do lead to non-unitary representations upon quantisations.
We believe that this is not the case for the following reasons.

The coadjoint orbits with
m<s
rather lead
to an unfamiliar class of
unitary
representations
which are not of the lowest energy type:
see our forthcoming paper
[
93
]
for explicit
construction of such representations.
Luckily, the
d=3
case can give us good lessons about this issue:
using the isomorphism
\mathfrak{so}(2,2)\simeq\mathfrak{so}(2,1)\oplus\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
,
we can decompose the
\mathfrak{so}(2,2)
orbit
as a product of two
\mathfrak{so}(2,1)
orbits
(see Appendix
E
for a dictionary).
The massive spinning particle orbits with
m>s
correspond
to the products of two elliptic hyperboloids
H^{2}_{\pm}

of radius
j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle L}=\frac{m+s}{2}>0
and
j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle R}=\frac{m-s}{2}>0

(see (
2.46
) for
the definition of
H^{2}_{\pm}
).
Since the
H^{2}_{\pm}(j^{2})
orbit corresponds
the lowest/highest weight representation
{\cal D}^{\pm}_{j}

with the lowest/highest weight
\pm j
, the massive
spinning orbits correspond to the representations

({\cal D}^{\pm}_{j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle L}}\otimes{\cal D}^{\pm}_{j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle R}})\oplus({\cal D}^{\pm}_{j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle R}}\otimes{\cal D}^{\pm}_{j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle L}})
.
At the level of representations, we find the decomposition,

{\cal D}^{\pm}_{-\frac{t-1}{2}}={\cal D}_{\frac{t-1}{2}}\oplus{\cal D}^{\pm}_{\frac{t+1}{2}}\,,

(6.28)

when
2j
becomes a non-positive integer
-(t-1)

with
t\geq 1
.
Here,
{\cal D}_{\frac{t-1}{2}}
is the
t
-dimensional representation.
And, for
m=s-t
, the quotient representations

({\cal D}^{\pm}_{s-\frac{t-1}{2}}\otimes{\cal D}_{\frac{t-1}{2}})\oplus({\cal D}_{\frac{t-1}{2}}\otimes{\cal D}^{\pm}_{s-\frac{t-1}{2}})\,,

(6.29)

correspond to partially massless representations of depth
t
,
which are non-unitary for
t>1
.
Here, the non-unitarity is due to the finite-dimensional
representation
{\cal D}_{\frac{t-1}{2}}
of
\mathfrak{so}(1,2)
algebra.
Therefore, one might confirm once again that the orbits
with
m=0,\ldots,s-2
lead to non-unitary representations.
However, this is not correct because the non-unitary
representation
{\cal D}_{\frac{t-1}{2}}
should arise from
S^{2}
,
while the orbits with
m=s-t+1
is given by
the product space,

\left[H^{2}_{\pm}\big((s-\tfrac{t-1}{2})^{2}\big)\times H^{2}_{\mp}\big((\tfrac{t-1}{2})^{2}\big)\right]\cup\left[H^{2}_{\mp}\big((\tfrac{t-1}{2})^{2}\big)\times H^{2}_{\pm}\big((s-\tfrac{t-1}{2})^{2}\big)\right]\,.

(6.30)

This coadjoint orbit would correspond
to the
unitary
representation,

({\cal D}^{\pm}_{s-\frac{t-1}{2}}\otimes{\cal D}^{\mp}_{\frac{t-1}{2}})\oplus({\cal D}^{\mp}_{\frac{t-1}{2}}\otimes{\cal D}^{\pm}_{s-\frac{t-1}{2}})\,,

(6.31)

whose particle interpretation is unclear
for the moment.
In Section
6.4
, we propose an interpretation
for this type of orbits.

We may understand this issue from a different angle:
the
O(2,2)
group has two discrete symmetries, the
time reversal sending
m\to-m
and the parity sending
s\to-s
.
In terms of
O(1,2)\times O(1,2)
it corresponds to
(j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle L},j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle R})\to(-j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle R},-j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle L})

and
(j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle L},j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle R})\to(j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle R},j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle L})
.
We can consider yet another automorphism sending
(j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle L},j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle R})\to(j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle L},-j_{\rm\scriptscriptstyle R})

or equivalently
(m,s)\to(s,m)
,
which is not an element of
O(2,2)
.
Note that this “inversion” — up to a dimension related shift which would arise upon quantisation — has been used within the context of conformal field theory
[
163
]
.
Therefore, the coadjoint orbits with
m<s
in any dimensions
would also correspond to unitary representations, which are somehow mixed
with the usual massive spinning particle through the inversion.

•

The coadjoint orbits with light-like momenta
and space-like spins are given by

\phi=E\,{\cal P}^{+}+s\,{\cal J}^{12}\,,\qquad\mathfrak{g}_{\phi}=\mathbb{R}_{P_{-}}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)_{J_{12}}\oplus\mathfrak{iso}(d-4)_{[-;3,\dots,d-2]}\,,

(6.32)

while, with the indices
\chi=1,\pi=2
, we find
that the dual orbit is characterised by

{\tilde{\phi}}=\sigma\,{\cal U}+{\cal S}^{\chi\chi}+s^{2}\,{\cal S}^{\pi\pi}\,,\qquad\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\mathbb{R}_{T}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)_{S_{\pi\pi}+s^{2}S_{\chi\chi}}\,.

(6.33)

The Casimir functions of this orbit read

C_{2}=-\sigma\,s^{2}\,,\qquad C_{4}=0\,.

(6.34)

In comparison with the Minkowski case, these (A)dS orbits
seem to be related to the massless spinning particles,
but we have already seen that for AdS, the massless spinning particle
is associated with
\phi=s\,{\cal P}^{0}+s\,{\cal J}^{12}
.
In fact, we see that the stabiliser

\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tilde{\phi}}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathfrak{u}(1)

is smaller than that of Minkowski,

\mathfrak{heis}_{2}\niplus\mathfrak{u}(1)
: the former
has dimension
2
and the latter has
4
. Therefore,
these orbits are too big for a massless spinning particle,
and they just correspond to the end point of the spectrum
of the massive and tachyonic spinning particles in dS and AdS,
respectively.

•

The coadjoint orbits with light-like momenta
and spins are given by

\phi=E\,{\cal P}^{+}+\epsilon\,{\cal J}^{-1}\,.

(6.35)

Here again,
E
and
\epsilon
are not separately good parameters
but the combination
\varepsilon^{2}=E\,\epsilon

is (we can always set
E\,\epsilon>0
by a suitable rotation).
By analogy with the Minkowski case,
the corresponding action can be interpreted as
the action for continuous spin particles in (A)dS.

In dS, the coadjoint vector (
6.39
)
actually belongs to a massive spinning orbits with

\varepsilon\in\sqrt{2}\,\mathbb{N}
. Rescaling
\phi

with
J_{+-}
we can set

\phi=\varepsilon\,\left({\cal J}^{\bullet+}+{\cal J}^{-1}\right)=\tfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\,\big({\cal J}^{\bullet 0}-{\cal J}^{10}+{\cal J}^{\bullet d-1}+{\cal J}^{1\,d-1}\big)\,.

(6.36)

Note here that only when the components
\bullet

and 1 have the same signature, that is only in dS,
we can perform a
\pi/2
-rotation
in the
\bullet
–
1
plane to get

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}\,\left({\cal J}^{\bullet 0}+{\cal J}^{1\,d-1}\right)

(6.37)

\displaystyle\simeq
